design a high-performance OpenMP matmul, correctness with external hash validation (via -r). The output hash must match original semantics? \\\"maintaining correctness and equivalent semantics to the original code\\\" — for external validation they probably compare hash of C. If we change summation order, floating point results change and hash differs. Hmm. This is likely evaluated against a reference output. The original code computes sum over k in order. If we parallelize over i,j with each thread computing full k loop, the per-element order remains identical, so bitwise identical results. That is safest. Parallelize the i loop (or collapse i,j), keeping k serial inner with vectorization. That gives identical results to reference.\\n\\nFor maximum performance, matrix multiply cache-friendly with k inner loop over B column is non-contiguous (B[k*N+j]). Original semantics use that order. To keep identical results, we must keep the k loop order per element. We can parallelize outer i and/or j. But for performance, we could transpose B — then the sum order over k is same but values same, so result bitwise identical (same additions in same order). Yes, transposing B and computing dot product over contiguous row of B^T yields exactly same operations in same k order. Great. But creating B^T copy is fine, one-time cost. Alternatively blocked with same k order still same additions order, regardless of blocking since each element's k sequence remains 0..N-1. So we can block k too while preserving order.\\n\\nBest: parallelize over rows (i), each row computing dot products, optionally collapse. Use loop order i (parallel), k (outer within block?), j inner. For each C[i][j], compute over k in order. If we do i-parallel, then j loop with k innermost, compiler vectorizes over j with rhs B[k*N+j] contiguous, A[i*N+k] broadcast. This is efficient and keeps per-element k order exactly. Auto-vectorization over j gives SIMD, but requires #pragma omp simd on the j loop maybe. The k loop order per element remains ascending. Floating point addition per element in same order -> identical results. Compiler vectorization over j keeps each lane's own accumulation, so identical.\\n\\nStructure:\\n\\n#pragma omp parallel for schedule(static) \\nfor i:\\n  for j: C[i*N+j]=0\\n  for k:\\n     double aik = A[i*N+k];\\n     #pragma omp simd\\n     for j: C[i*N+j] += aik * B[k*N+j];\\n\\nThis is effectively a saxpy-based matmul, vectorizes well, but the C row stays in cache. This yields same results as original and high performance. OpenMP parallel over i. For load balance static ideal.\\n\\nHowever, for N large, this is fine. Need to include <algorithm> for std::min. Write matrixMultiply.\n\nConsider performance tuning: N=512, 256 threads. Scheduling static over 512 iterations = 2 per thread; OpenMP overhead of fork/join with 256 threads could be significant but unavoidable. Fine.\n\nActually for scalability with 256 threads and small N, perhaps parallelize over rows with schedule(static). Good.\n\nBut there's a concern about using #pragma omp simd with size_t loop variable; GCC handles.\n\nLet me also think: is this kernel truly maximizing? For each i row, k loop, B row stream. Reads B = N^2*N? For each i, we read entire B (N^2) -> total N^3 reads of B (8 bytes each), that's enormous—worse than blocked. Original loop i,j,k reads B column-wise for each element, also N^3. Our version still reads all B for each row i: N * N^2 = N^3 elements. That's same as naive but sequential. Compute intensity: each B element is used for one FMA per i. Hmm, but it's streamed once per row: N^3/2 FMAs and N^3 loads. Arithmetic intensity ~ 2*N^3 / (N^3*8) = 0.25 FLOP/byte. That's memory bound! Bad.\n\nBetter approach: block over i too so that B block is reused for multiple rows. Classic register-blocked approach: process MR rows at a time, with C accumulators in registers across MR rows and vector lanes j. Kernel:\n\n#pragma omp parallel for schedule(static)\nfor (ii = 0; ii < N; ii += MB) {\n  for (jj = 0; jj < N; jj += NB) {\n     // zero C block\n     for (i in ii..ii+MB) for j in block C=0;\n     for (k = 0; k < N; ++k) {\n        for (i in block) {\n           aik = A[i*N+k];\n           for (j in block) C[i][j] += aik * B[k][j];\n        }\n     }\n  }\n}\n\nThis reuses B block for MB rows. C block MB*NB stays in L2. Each B element loaded once per C block, total loads N^3/MB. With MB=8, intensity 2 FLOP/byte... still modest but better. Better k blocking too to keep B panels in cache: standard tiling.\n\nAlternative transposed B dot product with register blocking over i and j gives high performance: compute C[i][j] with k inner contiguous over A row and Bt row; vectorize over k and accumulate. But k reduction vectorization with FMA changes grouping—still per-element order? With vectorization over k accumulation uses 4/8 partial sums and then horizontal add, changing rounding vs sequential. That would change hash. So avoid vectorizing over k.\n\nAlternative vectorize over i (rows) for a fixed j and k: C[i][j] += A[i][k]*B[k][j], i contiguous? A[i*N+k] strided by N. Not contiguous.\n\nVectorize over j is natural (B row contiguous, C row contiguous) and keeps k order. So blocking i,j and k outer, with inner j vectorized. Let's implement tiling with MB (rows) ~8, NB (cols) ~ 256, KB maybe 128 or full N? For B reuse and L2, k-blocking allows B panel KB x N and C MB x NB to stay cache. But per element k order still ascending, so bitwise identical. Great.\n\nDesign:\nconstexpr size_t MB = 8; // rows per block\nconstexpr size_t NB = 128; // cols per block\nconstexpr size_t KB = 128; // k block\n\nparallel for over ii (blocks of MB rows), schedule(static). Number of iterations N/MB, enough for 256 threads when N=512 => 64 iterations only; with 256 threads that's under-utilized (64 blocks < 256 threads). So need finer parallelism. Could collapse ii and jj loops: `#pragma omp parallel for collapse(2) schedule(static)` over ii and jj. Each (ii,jj) block independent. With N=512, MB=8, NB=128 => 64*4=256 iterations, perfect for 256 threads. For N=512 with 256 threads each block computes 8x128 output over k=512 => ~0.5M FLOP per thread; decent.
